--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:**
- Before switch time: robots should be **moving** (score penalizes stationary robots)
- After switch time: robots should be **stationary** (score penalizes moving robots)
- Switch signal: a **GREEN** wall block lights up at switch time
- So: robots should move until they see GREEN, then stop

**Key insight:** 
- Before switch: explore/move to minimize stationary penalty
- After switch: stop to minimize moving penalty
- Trigger: detect GREEN color (color 2 in the color set... wait, let me check: colors {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Green is likely color 2 or maybe color 3. In ARGoS, typical color mapping: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. So GREEN = 2)

**Basic 2-state machine:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to State 1 when GREEN perceived
- State 1: Stop → maybe transition back if no green (but we don't want to go back)

**Design considerations:**
- Need to detect green wall: use C=7 (perceives color) with color 2 (green)
- Once stopped, should stay stopped → low probability transition back or no transition back
- Could also use 3-state machines for robustness

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Best guess (2-state):** Explore → Stop on green detection
2. **Variants:** Different probabilities, transition structures
3. **3-state variants:** With intermediate states
4. **Exploration variants:** Different movement behaviors

For the green color, I'll use `--l<I>x<K> 2` (green = color index 2).

Wait - the problem says "one wall block lights up GREEN". The robots perceive objects with their camera (C=7). Green walls should be detectable.

Let me design the machines:

**State 0:** Exploration (B=0), transition to Stop on seeing green (C=7, l=2, p=0.9)
**State 1:** Stop (B=1), one transition back with very low prob (C=5, p=0.01) or stay

For state 1 (Stop), I'll add a very low probability of going back to avoid getting stuck if green was a false positive, but mainly stay stopped.

## Reasoning

**Fixing errors from Round 2:**
1. `--n1 0` means 0 transitions from state 1, but then `--n1x0` was still declared — fix: when `--n1 0`, don't declare any transitions from state 1.
2. `--cle<I>` only allows {0, 4, 5, 6} (not 1, 2, 3). Fix all LED colors.

**Mission recap:**
- Before switch: robots should MOVE (penalized for being stationary)
- After switch: robots should STOP (penalized for moving)
- Switch signal: GREEN wall lights up → detect with C=7, color=2 (green)

**Core strategy:**
- State 0: Exploration (B=0) — moving
- State 1: Stop (B=1) — stationary
- Transition 0→1: perceive green (C=7, l=2, high probability)
- State 1 should be a near-absorbing state (very low or no exit)

**Key fixes:**
- When `--n<I> 0`, declare NO transitions from that state
- LED colors only from {0, 4, 5, 6}

Let me carefully construct 14 valid machines:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The errors say:
1. `--n1x0` is missing its destination index — this means `--n1x0 D` where D is the destination among OTHER states. I need to make sure every transition has `--n<I>x<K> D` declared.
2. `--cle1` used invalid value '2' — but I thought I fixed this. Let me check my Round 3 FSMs again...

The problematic FSM from Round 3 is the last one:
```
--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 0
```
When `--n1 0`, there are NO transitions, so no `--n1x0` should be declared. BUT the error says `--n1x0` is missing destination. This suggests the parser is confused, OR the issue is elsewhere.

Wait — looking at FSM 11 (3-state): 
```
--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.002 --n1x1 1 --c1x1 7 --l1x1 2 --p1x1 0.01
```
Here `--n1x0 0` — destination 0. In a 3-state machine leaving state 1, the others are [0, 2], so D=0 means state 0 and D=1 means state 2. This looks correct.

And FSM 10 (3-state):
```
--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.003
```
In a 3-state machine leaving state 1, D=0→state 0, D=1→state 2. So `--n1x0 1` sends to state 2. That seems fine.

The last FSM:
```
--nstates 3 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 4 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 0 --s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 0 --n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.5
```
`--n1 0` means 0 transitions. But then `--s2` follows. Perhaps the parser expects `--n1x0` even when n=0? Or perhaps `--n2x0 0` — in a 3-state machine leaving state 2, others are [0,1], so D=0→state 0. That's fine.

Actually, I suspect the issue might be that `--n1 0` is not allowed — minimum transitions might be 1. Let me re-read: "integer in [1..4]" — YES! `--n<I> T` requires T in [1..4]. So `--n1 0` is INVALID. Every state must have at least 1 transition.

This was the bug: `--n1 0` is not allowed. Every state needs at least 1 transition.

Also the `--cle1 2` error — I had `--cle1 0` in all my Round 3 FSMs, so maybe the error is carried over from Round 2. Let me just be careful: only {0, 4, 5, 6} for LED.

**Fix:** Every state must have `--n<I>` in [1..4]. For the Stop state that should stay stopped, use a very low probability transition (C=5, p=0.001) back to exploration, or use C=7 (green perception) to re-enter stop state if it somehow exited.

Let me redesign carefully:

**2-state core design:**
- State 0: Exploration, transition to State 1 on green (C=7, l=2, p=0.9)
- State 1: Stop, transition to State 1... wait, can't self-loop. Must go to another state. Use very low prob transition back to State 0.

Actually with 2 states, from state 1 the only destination D=0 is state 0. So:
- State 1: Stop, `--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001` (very rarely goes back)

This is what I had. Let me verify FSM format more carefully.

For 3-state machine, state 1 leaving to others [0,2]: D=0→state0, D=1→state2.

Let me write 14 clean, valid FSMs:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing. Let me carefully trace through what's happening.

The error says `--n1x0` is missing its destination index. This means somewhere I'm writing `--n1x0` without the destination `D` value following it — OR the simulator is interpreting something wrongly.

Wait — I think I see it now. Looking at FSM like:
```
--n0x1 0 --c0x1 3 --p0x1 5 --w0x1 10.0
```
Here `--p0x1 5` — for condition C=3, p is an INTEGER neighbour count. The value `5` is correct. But could the parser be misreading `5` as a float and then `--w0x1` as the destination? No...

Let me look at the 3-state machines more carefully. In a 3-state machine:
- State 1's transitions: others are indexed [0, 2] → D=0 means state 0, D=1 means state 2
- `--n1x0 1` means transition 0 from state 1 goes to state 2 ✓
- `--n1x1 1` means transition 1 from state 1 goes to state 2 ✓

Actually wait — in FSM 12 (3-state):
```
--n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.002 --n1x1 1 --c1x1 7 --l1x1 2 --p1x1 0.01
```
`--n1x0 0` = destination 0 = state 0 ✓
`--n1x1 1` = destination 1 = state 2 ✓

That looks fine. Let me check FSM 10 (3-state):
```
--n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.003
```
`--n1x0 1` = destination 1 = state 2 ✓

Hmm. Let me look at the 4-state FSM:
```
--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.05
```
In a 4-state machine leaving state 1, others are [0, 2, 3] → D=0=state0, D=1=state2, D=2=state3.
`--n1x0 0` = destination 0 = state 0 ✓

Wait... maybe the issue is that `--n1x0` literally needs the destination written as the NEXT token, and something in my formatting is off. Let me look at the error more carefully: "Transition '--n1x0' is missing its destination index."

Could it be that in one of my FSMs I wrote `--n1x0` at the very end of a line without a value? Or that `--n1 0` (zero transitions) is causing the parser to expect `--n1x0`?

**AH WAIT.** The error says `--n1 0` is actually invalid (must be [1..4]), BUT more critically — could the issue be with the last FSM in Round 3/4:

```
--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.002
```

In the LAST FSM (3-state), state 1 with `--n1x0 1` — but wait, in a 3-state machine leaving state 1, the destination indices for OTHER states are 0 (→state0) and 1 (→state2). So D=1 → state 2. Then state 2 is `--s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 0 --n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.5`. From state 2, others are [0,1], D=0→state0. That's fine.

**NEW HYPOTHESIS:** Maybe the `--cle1 2` error and `--n1x0 missing destination` errors are from machines in the POPULATION that I didn't write in round 4 but were carried over. OR — the simulator error messages are cumulative and refer to machines from earlier rounds.

Actually, re-reading: "ERRORS DETECTED IN YOUR PREVIOUS ANSWERS" — this means errors from what I submitted. But I thought I fixed `--cle 2`. Let me check ALL my Round 4 FSMs for `--cle` values... I see `--cle0 4` in one FSM and `--cle2 0` elsewhere. No `--cle1 2`... 

OH WAIT. In FSM 12 from Round 4:
```
--n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.002 --n1x1 1 --c1x1 7 --l1x1 2 --p1x1 0.01
```
`--l1x1 2` — this is the color parameter for condition C=7. Color 2 = green. This is FINE, it's `--l` not `--cle`.

Hmm, I'm stumped. Let me try a completely different approach and use only the SIMPLEST possible FSMs to avoid any parsing issues, and see if the errors go away.

Actually — I just realized: could the issue be `--clr2 2` in GoToColor? `--clr<I>` takes values {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Color 2 should be valid.

Let me try to avoid GoToColor (B=8) and any 3+ state machines with complex transitions for now, and focus on very simple 2-state machines. I'll also avoid any machine where state 1 has anything other than a dead-simple single transition.

For the `--cle1 2` error — I genuinely don't see where I wrote it in Round 4. Unless it's from Round 3's last FSM that I carried forward conceptually... In Round 3 I had:
```
--s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 0
```
But `--n1 0` is invalid (must be ≥1). So the parser might be misreading the next token (which would be `--s2`) as part of state 1's transitions, getting confused.

**CONCLUSION:** The root cause is `--n1 0` being invalid. When I write `--n1 0`, the parser might try to read `--s2` as `--n1x0 [destination]` and fail. This would explain BOTH errors simultaneously:
- "missing destination" because it tries to parse `--s2` as the destination of `--n1x0`
- Some other cascading parse error

I fixed `--n1 0` in Round 4 (every state has n≥1), so why do errors persist? Let me check if I accidentally carried a bad FSM...

In Round 4, the 3-state machine (FSM 13/last):
```
--nstates 3 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 4 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 1 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.002 --s2 8 --vel2 1.0 --clr2 2 --cle2 0 --n2 1 --n2x0 0 --c2x0 5 --p2x0 0.5
```

This looks syntactically correct to me. But maybe `--clr2 2` is being misread? Let me try green = color index... Actually in the problem statement: "one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}" and the wall is GREEN. What if green is actually color index 3 or something else? Let me try different color indices for green.

Actually, I'll just produce clean, minimal FSMs and avoid B=8 (GoToColor) entirely for now to eliminate that variable. I'll also make sure every state has exactly 1 transition with a simple condition.

Let me produce 14 very clean FSMs:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same two errors keep appearing despite my fixes. Let me think very carefully about what could be causing them persistently.

**Error 1: `--n1x0` missing destination index**
**Error 2: `--cle1` invalid value 2**

I have been very careful not to write `--cle1 2` or `--n1x0` without a destination. Yet the errors persist. 

Wait — could the error message be referring to errors from the *entire history* of submissions, not just the most recent round? Or could it be that these errors exist in machines that were accepted into the population from previous rounds and are being re-evaluated?

OR — could the issue be something subtler? Let me look at this FSM from Round 5:
```
--n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 7 --l0x1 2 --p0x1 0.9
```
Two transitions from state 0, both going to destination 0 (state 1). Both are C=7 with l=2. This is redundant but syntactically valid...

Actually, I wonder if the error comes from `--n0x1 0` where having two transitions with the same destination might cause issues.

Let me look at another possibility: the `--l1x0 2` parameter in:
```
--n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --l1x0 2 --p1x0 0.005
```
Could `--l1x0 2` be misread as `--cle` something? No, `--l` and `--cle` are different.

**New theory:** Could the error be in how I'm writing `--n0x1 0`? In a 2-state machine, from state 0, the only other state is state 1. So the destination D must be 0 (meaning "the first of the other states" = state 1). So `--n0x1 0` is correct for a 2-state machine.

But what if having TWO transitions both with D=0 in a 2-state machine is illegal? Let me avoid that.

**Most likely real issue:** I think the error reporting might be cumulative or referring to something from the initial population (Round 1) that I didn't generate. Since I was told "Round 1 has just completed" before I submitted anything, there was already a Round 1 population. The errors might be from that pre-existing population being mixed in.

OR — the system might be flagging errors from machines that persist in the population from my earlier submissions. Since the problem says "SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim", maybe the best machine has an error? But the best machine would have been evaluated already...

I think the safest approach is to generate FSMs that are as simple and unambiguous as possible. Let me try using only:
- 2-state machines
- State 0: single transition (C=7, green detection)
- State 1: single transition (C=5, tiny probability)
- No duplicate transition destinations
- No complex multi-transition states

Also: I've been assuming green = color 2. What if the color indices are different? Let me try color 3 as well (could be green in some mappings).

Let me write 14 extremely simple, clean FSMs:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

These exact same two errors keep appearing every round despite my best efforts to fix them. I need to think about this differently.

**The errors are:**
1. `Transition '--n1x0' is missing its destination index`
2. `'--cle1' used the invalid value '2'`

I have been writing `--n1x0 0` (with destination 0) and `--cle1 0` or `--cle1 4` in all my Round 5 and 6 submissions. I am NOT writing these errors. So either:

1. The error messages are **stale/cumulative** from earlier rounds and don't reflect my current submissions
2. There is a **pre-existing population** from Round 1 that contains these errors, and they persist in the pool
3. The errors are in machines I'm not aware of

Since I cannot control the pre-existing machines, and since my own machines appear syntactically correct, I should just continue submitting valid machines and not worry about these persistent error messages.

**What I should focus on:** Did any of my machines actually run and score well? I haven't received score feedback, which suggests either the machines are running but I'm not being told scores, or all are being rejected.

Given that the budget is decreasing (600→595→590→584→577→568), simulations ARE being consumed, so machines ARE running.

**Strategy:** Continue with the core 2-state design (Explore→Stop on green). The design is logically sound. Let me also try variations that might work better:

1. Maybe the robots can't see the green wall from everywhere — try GoToColor(green) as an intermediate state
2. Try different color indices for green (1,2,3,4,5,6)
3. Try a machine that starts stopped and moves until it sees green (wrong strategy but exploratory)
4. Try machines where stop state has C=7 green transition back to itself (can't self-loop, but could go to explore briefly)

Actually, let me reconsider the color for GREEN. The problem says "lights up GREEN". In ARGoS color conventions, the color indices for `--clr` and `--l` parameters might map as:
- 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

So green = 2. I've been using this. Let me keep it.

For 13 machines, let me produce clean 2-state FSMs with only the simplest structure. I'll write them out extremely carefully, character by character.

**Template for each FSM:**
```
--nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 [R] --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 [P] --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 [Q]
```

Where:
- `--n0x0 0`: from state 0, transition 0 goes to destination index 0 (= state 1, the only other state)
- `--n1x0 0`: from state 1, transition 0 goes to destination index 0 (= state 0, the only other state)
- All `--cle` values are 0 (valid)

This is the simplest possible valid structure. Let me vary R (turning rate), P (green detection probability), and Q (return probability).

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same errors keep appearing. Since my FSMs clearly do include `--n1x0 0` (with destination 0) and never use `--cle1 2`, I now believe these error messages are **stale/persistent** from earlier rounds (Rounds 1-3) and are being reported again as a reminder, not as new errors from my Round 7 submission. My Round 5-7 FSMs appear syntactically correct.

The machines ARE running (budget decreasing), so I should focus on **what strategy works best** rather than the error messages.

**Strategy reflection:**
- Core design: Explore (B=0) → Stop (B=1) when green (C=7, l=2) is detected
- This is logically correct for the mission
- The machines are running but I don't know scores
- Maybe the issue is that not all robots can see the green wall (it's on a wall, range-and-bearing is 0.5m, camera detects colored objects)
- The camera in e-puck detects colored OBJECTS (including walls) in the visual field
- So robots near the green wall see it, but robots far away may not

**Key insight I may have missed:** The green wall is only visible to robots near it. Robots far from the green wall won't detect it and won't stop. This means the green-detection strategy only works well if robots spread out enough to have at least some near the wall, AND if there's a way to propagate the "stop" signal.

**Alternative approaches:**
1. Use neighbor count: when robots stop (seeing green), others see fewer moving neighbors... but that's indirect
2. Use a fixed timer: stop after ~500 steps (midpoint of 400-600 range) — but no timer exists
3. Use probability-based transition with very low p to eventually stop — but this is random timing
4. Combine green detection + neighbor-based propagation

Actually, let me reconsider: robots with LED colors on could signal to neighbors. If stopped robots emit a color (e.g., yellow=4), moving robots could detect that color (C=7, l=4) and stop too. This creates a cascade effect!

**Cascade design (3-state):**
- State 0: Explore, LED=off → transition to State 1 on green (C=7, l=2)
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow(4) → stays stopped (very low p exit)
- This way, robots that see the yellow LED of stopped robots could also stop

But wait — can robots detect other robots' LEDs via C=7? The condition C=7 says "the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera". This should include other robots' LED colors within camera range.

**Cascade 3-state design:**
- State 0: Explore, LED=off → transition to State 1 when sees green(2) OR sees yellow(4) from stopped robots
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow(4) → very rarely exits

Let me try this cascade approach alongside the simple 2-state approach.

For 13 machines, I'll do:
- 6 simple 2-state (elitism + variants)
- 4 cascade 2-state (detect yellow from stopped peers)  
- 3 cascade 3-state

For the cascade approach in 2-state:
- State 0: Explore → Stop when sees green(2) [p=0.9] OR sees yellow(4) [p=0.9]
- But can only have one C=7 per transition... wait, I can have 2 transitions both going to state 1

Actually with `--n0 2`:
- transition 0: C=7, l=2, p=0.9 → dest 0 (state 1)
- transition 1: C=7, l=4, p=0.9 → dest 0 (state 1)

And state 0 LED = 0 (off), state 1 LED = 4 (yellow), state 1 stops.

Let me write these carefully:

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 10 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --l0x0 2 --p0x0 0.9 --s1 1 --cle1 0 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.005

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The same errors persist across all rounds. Since my FSMs clearly contain `--n1x0 0` (with destination) and never use `--cle1 2`, I'm now confident these are **stale error messages** from the very early rounds (1-3) that keep being reported. My machines from rounds 5-8 are syntactically valid and ARE running (budget depletes).

Since I have no score feedback, I need to reason about what's working. The core strategy (Explore→Stop on green) is sound. Let me think about what might be limiting performance:

1. **Green detection range**: The camera sees colored objects. The green wall is a wall segment. Robots must be close enough and facing the right direction to see it.
2. **Cascade propagation**: Robots near the green wall stop first, then neighbors can detect their LED color and also stop.
3. **Coverage before switch**: Robots should spread out to be near the green wall wherever it appears.

**Key improvements to try:**
- **Repulsion behavior** in state 0: spreads robots out better than exploration, so more robots are near the green wall when it lights up
- **GoToColor(green)**: actively move toward green when seen, then stop — but this might overshoot
- **Stronger cascade**: use magenta(5) as the "I stopped" signal since yellow(4) might conflict
- **Multiple color detection**: try detecting all possible colors that might be green

Let me also reconsider: maybe the issue is that after detecting green, robots should **GoToColor(green)** briefly to get closer to the wall, then stop — but actually stopping near the wall is fine, the goal is just to stop.

Actually, **repulsion** before switch is good: spreads robots so at least some are always near the green wall. After switch, stop.

Let me design:
- **Type A**: Repulsion → Stop on green (spread out, then stop)
- **Type B**: Explore → Stop on green (baseline)  
- **Type C**: Explore → Stop on green OR yellow (cascade)
- **Type D**: Repulsion → Stop on green OR yellow (cascade + spread)

For 3-state machines with repulsion:
- State 0: Repulsion (spreads robots), LED=off → Stop on green
- State 1: Stop, LED=yellow → tiny prob back

## Reasoning

- Before switch (~400-600 steps): be MOVING → use Exploration or Repulsion
- After switch: be STOPPED → use Stop
- Signal: GREEN wall lights up → detect with C=7, l=2

**What I've tried:**
- Simple 2-state: Explore→Stop on green (most of rounds 5-9)
- Cascade: stopped robots emit yellow(4)/magenta(5), moving robots detect peer LEDs
- Repulsion instead of exploration (spreads robots to cover arena better)
- Various probabilities and turning rates

**For the final round, I should focus on the most promising designs:**

The cascade approach (stopped robots signal peers via LED) is theoretically superior because it propagates the stop signal even to robots that can't directly see the green wall. The key insight is:
- Robot near green wall → sees green → stops → emits yellow LED
- Robot near stopped robot → sees yellow → stops → emits yellow LED
- This creates a wave of stopping

The repulsion behavior helps spread robots uniformly, so at least some will be near the green wall.

**Best candidate designs:**

1. **Repulsion + cascade** (2-state): Repel to spread, stop when see green OR yellow, emit yellow when stopped
2. **Explore + cascade** (2-state): Explore, stop when see green OR yellow, emit yellow
3. **Simple reliable** (2-state): Explore, stop when see green, p=0.9-1.0

For the final 12 machines, I'll weight toward cascade+repulsion variants with fine-tuned parameters, plus keep the simple baseline for elitism.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
